def generate_grid_fixed(max_x, max_y):
    cells = [(x,y) for x in range(max_x+1) for y in range(max_y+1)]
    # order: maybe row-major
    cells.sort(key=lambda p: (p[0]+p[1], p[0]))  # BFS order to reduce branching
    assignments = []
    def backtrack(idx, grid):
        if idx == len(cells):
            assignments.append(dict(grid))
            return
        x,y = cells[idx]
        if (x,y)==(0,0):
            grid[(0,0)] = 0
            backtrack(idx+1, grid)
            del grid[(0,0)]
            return
        # compute possible values bound
        max_val = 2*(max_x+max_y)+5
        possible = []
        for val in range(max_val+1):
            ok=True
            if (x-1,y) in grid:
                if abs(val - grid[(x-1,y)]) not in (1,2):
                    ok=False
            if (x,y-1) in grid:
                if abs(val - grid[(x,y-1)]) not in (1,2):
                    ok=False
            if not ok:
                continue
            # triple checks
            if (x, y+1) in grid and (x+1, y) in grid:
                vals = [val, grid[(x, y+1)], grid[(x+1, y)]]
                mn = min(vals)
                if sorted(vals) != [mn, mn+1, mn+2]:
                    ok=False
            if ok and (x, y-1) in grid and (x+1, y-1) in grid:
                vals = [grid[(x, y-1)], val, grid[(x+1, y-1)]]
                mn = min(vals)
                if sorted(vals) != [mn, mn+1, mn+2]:
                    ok=False
            if ok and (x-1, y) in grid and (x-1, y+1) in grid:
                vals = [grid[(x-1, y)], grid[(x-1, y+1)], val]
                mn = min(vals)
                if sorted(vals) != [mn, mn+1, mn+2]:
                    ok=False
            if ok:
                possible.append(val)
        for val in possible:
            grid[(x,y)] = val
            backtrack(idx+1, grid)
            del grid[(x,y)]
    backtrack(0, {})
    return assignments

[CODE] (turn 37):
def matches_linear(grid):
    # check if residues match either linear pattern (mod 3)
    for pattern in [(lambda x,y: (x+2*y)%3), (lambda x,y: (2*x+y)%3)]:
        if all(grid[(x,y)]%3 == pattern(x,y) for (x,y) in grid:
            return True
    return False

all_match = all(matches_linear(a) for a in assign5)
all_match

def matches_linear(grid):
    for pattern in [(lambda x,y: (x+2*y)%3), (lambda x,y: (2*x+y)%3)]:
        ok=True
        for (x,y),v in grid.items():
            if v%3 != pattern(x,y):
                ok=False
                break
        if ok:
            return True
    return False

[REASONING] (turn 41):
Great! All 318k assignments for 5x5 satisfy residues matching one of the two linear patterns. So the coloring modulo 3 is forced to be linear (either pattern). This suggests that for any infinite solution, the residues must follow one of the two linear forms (maybe globally). So we have two families of solutions modulo 3.

Now we need to consider actual integer values, not just residues. The condition also requires that the three numbers are consecutive, not just distinct residues. So beyond residues, the actual values must be such that each triple is a permutation of (n, n+1, n+2). This imposes ordering constraints.

Given residues pattern, we can think of constructing g as linear base plus a multiple of 3 times some integer function h(x,y) that is constant across each cell? Let's attempt.

Suppose we have a base linear function L(x,y) = x + 2y (or 2x + y). Then any solution g can be expressed as g(x,y) = L(x,y) + 3 * f(x,y), where f(x,y) is a nonnegative integer function (maybe any) that satisfies some constraints ensuring consecutive property.

Because L(x,y) already gives residues correctly (mod 3). Adding multiples of 3 does not change residues. The condition that the three values are consecutive numbers translates to: For each triple, the set {L + 3f} must be three consecutive integers. Since L values differ by 1 or 2 (mod 3) already, the base L values themselves are already three consecutive numbers? Let's check: For pattern L(x,y) = x+2y, we have L(x,y), L(x,y+1) = L+2, L(x+1,y) = L+1. Indeed they are exactly consecutive: L, L+1, L+2 (order maybe). So the base linear function already satisfies the condition with n = L(x,y). Indeed for L, the triple is exactly {L, L+1, L+2}. So L itself is a solution (with n = L). Similarly for L2 = 2x + y.

Now if we add 3k to each value, the triple becomes {L+3a, L+1+3b, L+2+3c} where a,b,c are integers (nonnegative maybe). For this set to be consecutive numbers, we need that after adding multiples of 3, the three numbers still form three consecutive integers. Since adding multiples of 3 shifts each element by a multiple of 3, the differences between them remain the same modulo 3, but the actual differences could become larger than 2. For them to be consecutive, the differences must be exactly 1 and 2 (in some order). Therefore the added multiples must be such that after adding, the three numbers are still consecutive. That imposes constraints on a,b,c.

Specifically, we need there exists n such that {L+3a, L+1+3b, L+2+3c} = {n, n+1, n+2}. Since the residues modulo 3 of these three numbers are 0,1,2 respectively (since L mod3 = 0? Wait L = x+2y. Mod 3, L can be 0,1,2 depending on x,y. But the residues of the three numbers are L mod3, (L+1) mod3, (L+2) mod3 which are distinct. Adding multiples of 3 doesn't change residues. So the residues condition is satisfied automatically. The only remaining condition is that the three numbers are consecutive, i.e., after sorting, they differ by 1. Since residues are distinct, the only way they can be consecutive is if the three numbers are exactly L+3a, L+1+3b, L+2+3c where the values after sorting are consecutive. Let's denote the smallest among them as n. Since residues are distinct, the ordering of residues determines which is smallest. The residues are 0,1,2 (mod 3) but actual values may shift due to the added multiples of 3.

Suppose we have a,b,c integers (maybe nonnegative). Then the three numbers are:

A = L + 3a
B = L+1 + 3b
C = L+2 + 3c

We need these three numbers to be consecutive. Let's sort them. Since residues are distinct, the order of residues determines the order of numbers modulo 3, but the added multiples of 3 could shift them.

Observation: If a,b,c are all equal, say k, then we get L+3k, L+1+3k, L+2+3k which are consecutive (just shifted by 3k). So any constant addition works.

If they are not equal, we need to see if still possible. For example, suppose a = k, b = k, c = k+1. Then numbers are L+3k, L+1+3k, L+2+3(k+1) = L+2+3k+3 = L+5+3k. That's gap of 4 between L+1+3k and L+5+3k, not consecutive. So not allowed.

Thus to keep them consecutive, we must have a = b = c (all equal). Because any difference would cause at least one gap >2.

Let's verify: Suppose a = b = c = k + d_i where d_i maybe differ. The numbers become L + 3k + 3d_a, L+1+3k+3d_b, L+2+3k+3d_c. The differences between them are (1 + 3(d_b - d_a)) and (1 + 3(d_c - d_b)). For them to be 1 and 2 (or 2 and 1) after sorting, the differences must be 1 and 2 (or 2 and 1). Since 3 times integer difference plus 1 yields numbers congruent to 1 mod 3. The only way to get difference 1 or 2 is when the integer differences are 0 (giving diff 1) or -1 (giving diff -2?) Wait compute diff between B and A: B - A = (L+1+3b) - (L+3a) = 1 + 3(b-a). For this to be either 1 or 2 (or -1 or -2 depending ordering), we need 3(b-a) to be 0 or 1 or -1? Actually difference could be negative if A > B. But absolute difference must be 1 or 2. So 1 + 3(b-a) must be in {±1, ±2}. Let's consider possibilities:

Case 1: B > A, then diff = 1 + 3(b-a) ∈ {1,2}. So 3(b-a) ∈ {0,1}. Since 3(b-a) is multiple of 3, the only possible is 0. So b-a = 0.

Case 2: B < A, then diff = -(1 + 3(b-a)) ∈ {1,2}. So 1 + 3(b-a) ∈ {-1,-2}. So 3(b-a) ∈ {-2,-3}. Only multiple of 3 among these is -3, giving b-a = -1. Then 1 + 3(-1) = -2, diff = 2 (since absolute). So if b = a-1, then B = L+1+3(a-1) = L+1+3a-3 = (L+3a) -2 = A -2. So B is 2 less than A. That's allowed (since consecutive numbers could be A-2, A-1, A). So B could be the smallest, A middle, C largest perhaps.

Similarly for C relative to B.

Thus the differences can be 1 or 2, but we need the three numbers to be consecutive, meaning after ordering they are n, n+1, n+2. This allows some of the numbers to be shifted down by 2 relative to base.

Let's analyze general condition: There exist integers a,b,c such that the set {L+3a, L+1+3b, L+2+3c} equals {n, n+1, n+2}. This is equivalent to there exists integer k such that after sorting, they are consecutive. Let's attempt to solve for a,b,c.

Let’s denote the three numbers as A, B, C as above. Since residues are distinct, the ordering of residues (mod 3) is fixed: the number congruent to L mod 3 is A, the one congruent to L+1 mod3 is B, the one congruent to L+2 mod3 is C. In the set of three consecutive integers, the residues modulo 3 are also distinct and appear in order (n mod3, n+1 mod3, n+2 mod3). So the mapping from residues to positions (smallest, middle, largest) is determined by n mod3 relative to L mod3.

Thus the ordering of A,B,C depends on the offset between n and L.

Specifically, suppose n ≡ L (mod 3). Then the smallest number (n) has same residue as L, i.e., A is smallest. Then the set would be {A, A+1, A+2} meaning B = A+1, C = A+2. That requires B - A = 1 and C - B = 1. Compute B - A = 1 + 3(b-a) = 1 => b = a. Compute C - B = 1 + 3(c-b) = 1 => c = b. So a=b=c. So constant addition.

If n ≡ L+1 (mod3), then the smallest number has residue L+1, i.e., B is smallest. Then ordering: B = n, C = n+1, A = n+2. So B smallest, C middle, A largest. Then we need C - B = 1 and A - C = 1.

Compute C - B = (L+2+3c) - (L+1+3b) = 1 + 3(c-b) = 1 => c = b.

Thus we have a solution where a = b+1, c = b. Similarly, there is case where n ≡ L+2 (mod3), then C is smallest, etc. That will give another pattern: c = b-1? Let's compute.

If n ≡ L+2 (mod3), then smallest residue is L+2, i.e., C is smallest. Then ordering: C = n, A = n+1, B = n+2. So C smallest, A middle, B largest. Conditions:

Let's attempt to characterize p more concretely.

Consider the differences: d_h(x,y) = p(x+1,y) - p(x,y). Similarly d_v(x,y) = p(x,y+1) - p(x,y). Since p values differ by at most 1, each difference is in {-1,0,1}. - all equal: a=b=c=k.
- a = k+1, b = k, c = k (i.e., a is larger by 1).
- a = k, b = k+1, c = k+1 (i.e., a is smaller by 1).

Thus the possibilities correspond to either a = b = c, or a - b = 1 and a - c = 1 (a larger), or b - a = 1 and c - a = 1 (a smaller). So differences:

Case 1: d_h = 0, d_v = 0.
Case 2: d_h = -1? Wait a larger than b => b = a-1 => d_h = b - a = -1. Similarly c = a-1 => d_v = -1. So both differences -1.
Case 3: a smaller than b and c => b = a+1 => d_h = 1, c = a+1 => d_v = 1.

Thus the condition simplifies to: For each (x,y), the pair (d_h(x,y), d_v(x,y)) must be either (0,0), (-1,-1), or (1,1). So the horizontal and vertical increments at a given point must be equal and can be -1, 0, or 1.

Thus p is a function where moving right or up from a point changes p by the same amount (either -1, 0, or 1), and this amount is consistent for both directions at that point.

p(x+1,y) = p(x,y) + s(x,y)
p(x,y+1) = p(x,y) + s(x,y)

Thus s(x,y) determines the increment for both right and up steps from (x,y). This also implies that p(x+1,y+1) can be reached via two paths: from (x+1,y) up (increment s(x+1,y)) or from (x,y+1) right (increment s(x,y+1)). Consistency requires:

p(x+1,y+1) = p(x+1,y) + s(x+1,y) = p(x,y) + s(x,y) + s(x+1,y)
Also = p(x,y+1) + s(x,y+1) = p(x,y) + s(x,y) + s(x,y+1)

def count_assignments(max_x, max_y):
    # grid size inclusive 0..max_x, 0..max_y
    # We'll store dict of (x,y)->value
    assignments = {}
    # We'll enforce condition for each L shape where all three points assigned
    # Use backtracking order row by row.
    points = [(x,y) for x in range(max_x+1) for y in range(max_y+1)]
    # sort maybe by sum x+y
    points.sort(key=lambda p: (p[0]+p[1], p[0]))
    solutions = []
    def backtrack(idx):
        if idx==len(points):
            solutions.append(dict(assignments))
            return
        x,y = points[idx]
        # Determine possible values based on constraints with already assigned neighbors
        # We'll compute lower bound 0 maybe.
        possible = set()
        # We can bound values maybe 0..some max
        # For simplicity, try values up to some limit.
        # Use constraints from any L shape where this point is part and other two already assigned.
        # L shapes: (x,y) with (x,y+1),(x+1,y)
        # (x-1,y) with (x-1,y+1),(x,y)   (if x>0)
        # (x,y-1) with (x+1,y-1),(x,y)   (if y>0)
        # We'll generate constraints requiring set of three to be consecutive.
        # For each L shape where this point is among the three and the other two are assigned,
        # we can deduce possible values.
        # We'll collect allowed values that satisfy all such constraints.
        # For each L shape, we have two assigned values a,b. The third value must be such that {a,b,third} are three consecutive ints.
        # That means third must be either min(a,b)-1, max(a,b)+1, or the missing middle value if |a-b|==2.
        # Let's compute set of possible third values for each shape and intersect.
        possible_vals = None
        # define helper to add constraint
        def constrain(known_vals):
            nonlocal possible_vals
            a,b = known_vals
            s = set()
            if a==b:
                # not allowed because must be distinct; but this shouldn't happen if consistent
                return False
            # compute possible third values
            mn = min(a,b)
            mx = max(a,b)
            if mx - mn == 1:
                # they are consecutive, missing third is either mn-1 or mx+1
                s.update([mn-1, mx+1])
            elif mx - mn == 2:
                # they are two apart, missing third is the middle value
                s.add(mn+1)
            else:
                # difference >2 impossible
                return False
            if possible_vals is None:
                possible_vals = s
            else:
                possible_vals = possible_vals.intersection(s)
                if not possible_vals:
                    return False
            return True
        
        # L shape with (x,y) as top-left? Actually shape includes (x,y), (x,y+1), (x+1,y)
        # Check if other two assigned:
        if (x, y+1) in assignments and (x+1, y) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x, y+1)], assignments[(x+1, y)])):
                return
        # shape where (x-1,y) is the (x,y) of condition, includes (x-1,y), (x-1,y+1), (x,y)
        if x>0 and (x-1, y) in assignments and (x-1, y+1) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x-1, y)], assignments[(x-1, y+1)])):
                return
        # shape where (x, y-1) is the (x,y) of condition, includes (x, y-1), (x, y), (x+1, y-1)
        if y>0 and (x, y-1) in assignments and (x+1, y-1) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x, y-1)], assignments[(x+1, y-1)])):
                return
        
        # If possible_vals is None (no constraints yet), we can try a range.
        if possible_vals is None:
            # bound maybe up to some max, but we can just try values from 0..max_x+max_y+5
            possible = range(0, max_x+max_y+10)
        else:
            possible = possible_vals
        for val in possible:
            if val < 0:
                continue
            assignments[(x,y)] = val
            # Also need to check that any L shape now fully assigned satisfies condition (i.e., set consecutive)
            # We'll verify after assignment for all L shapes that involve this point where all three are assigned.
            ok = True
            # list of L shapes to check
            shapes = []
            # shape with (x,y) as top-left
            shapes.append(((x,y),(x,y+1),(x+1,y)))
            # shape with (x-1,y) as top-left
            if x>0:
                shapes.append(((x-1,y),(x-1,y+1),(x,y)))
            # shape with (x,y-1) as top-left
            if y>0:
                shapes.append(((x,y-1),(x,y),(x+1,y-1)))
            for p1,p2,p3 in shapes:
                if p1 in assignments and p2 in assignments and p3 in assignments:
                    vals = [assignments[p1], assignments[p2], assignments[p3]]
                    mn = min(vals); mx = max(vals)
                    if mx - mn != 2 or len(set(vals))!=3:
                        ok=False
                        break
            if ok:
                backtrack(idx+1)
            del assignments[(x,y)]
        # end loop
    # set g(0,0)=0
    assignments[(0,0)] = 0
    backtrack(1)  # start from next point
    return solutions

sols = count_assignments(2,2)
len(sols)

        # That means third must be either min(a,b)-1, max(a,b)+1, or the missing middle value if |a-b|==2.
        # Let's compute set of possible third values for each shape and intersect.
        possible_vals = None
        # define helper to add constraint
        def constrain(known_vals):
            nonlocal possible_vals
            a,b = known_vals
            s = set()
            if a==b:
                # not allowed because must be distinct; but this shouldn't happen if consistent
                return False
            # compute possible third values
            mn = min(a,b)
            mx = max(a,b)
            if mx - mn == 1:
                # they are consecutive, missing third is either mn-1 or mx+1
                s.update([mn-1, mx+1])
            elif mx - mn == 2:
                # they are two apart, missing third is the middle value
                s.add(mn+1)
            else:
                # difference >2 impossible
                return False
            if possible_vals is None:
                possible_vals = s
            else:
                possible_vals = possible_vals.intersection(s)
                if not possible_vals:
                    return False
            return True
        
        # L shape with (x,y) as top-left? Actually shape includes (x,y), (x,y+1), (x+1,y)
        # Check if other two assigned:
        if (x, y+1) in assignments and (x+1, y) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x, y+1)], assignments[(x+1, y)])):
                return
        # shape where (x-1,y) is the (x,y) of condition, includes (x-1,y), (x-1,y+1), (x,y)
        if x>0 and (x-1, y) in assignments and (x-1, y+1) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x-1, y)], assignments[(x-1, y+1)])):
                return
        # shape where (x, y-1) is the (x,y) of condition, includes (x, y-1), (x, y), (x+1, y-1)
        if y>0 and (x, y-1) in assignments and (x+1, y-1) in assignments:
            if not constrain((assignments[(x, y-1)], assignments[(x+1, y-1)])):
                return
        
        # If possible_vals is None (no constraints yet), we can try a range.
        if possible_vals is None:
            # bound maybe up to some max, but we can just try values from 0..max_x+max_y+5
            possible = range(0, max_x+max_y+10)
        else:
            possible = possible_vals
        for val in possible:
            if val < 0:
                continue
            assignments[(x,y)] = val
            # Also need to check that any L shape now fully assigned satisfies condition (i.e., set consecutive)
            # We'll verify after assignment for all L shapes that involve this point where all three are assigned.
            ok = True
            # list of L shapes to check
            shapes = []
            # shape with (x,y) as top-left
            shapes.append(((x,y),(x,y+1),(x+1,y)))
            # shape with (x-1,y) as top-left
            if x>0:
                shapes.append(((x-1,y),(x-1,y+1),(x,y)))
            # shape with (x,y-1) as top-left
            if y>0:
                shapes.append(((x,y-1),(x,y),(x+1,y-1)))
            for p1,p2,p3 in shapes:
                if p1 in assignments and p2 in assignments and p3 in assignments:
                    vals = [assignments[p1], assignments[p2], assignments[p3]]
                    mn = min(vals); mx = max(vals)
                    if mx - mn != 2 or len(set(vals))!=3:
                        ok=False
                        break
            if ok:
                backtrack(idx+1)
            del assignments[(x,y)]
        # end loop
    # set g(0,0)=0
    assignments[(0,0)] = 0
    backtrack(1)  # start from next point
    return solutions

sols = count_assignments(2,2)
len(sols)

[CODE] (turn 7):
def count_assignments(max_x, max_y, max_val=30):
    assignments = {}
    points = [(x,y) for x in range(max_x+1) for y in range(max_y+1)]
    points.sort(key=lambda p: (p[0]+p[1], p[0]))
    solutions = []
    def backtrack(idx):
        if idx==len(points):
            solutions.append(dict(assignments))
            return
        x,y = points[idx]
        # compute possible values based on constraints
        possible_vals = None
        def constrain(a,b):
            nonlocal possible_vals
            if a==b:
                return False
            mn=min(a,b); mx=max(a,b)
            s=set()
            if mx-mn==1:
                s.update([mn-1,mx+1])
            elif mx-mn==2:
                s.add(mn+1)
            else:
                return False
            if possible_vals is None:
                possible_vals=s
            else:
                possible_vals=possible_vals.intersection(s)
                if not possible_vals:
                    return False
            return True
        # check shapes
        if (x, y+1) in assignments and (x+1, y) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x, y+1)], assignments[(x+1, y)]):
                return
        if x>0 and (x-1, y) in assignments and (x-1, y+1) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x-1, y)], assignments[(x-1, y+1)]):
                return
        if y>0 and (x, y-1) in assignments and (x+1, y-1) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x, y-1)], assignments[(x+1, y-1)]):
                return
        if possible_vals is None:
            possible = range(0, max_val+1)
        else:
            possible = [v for v in possible_vals if 0 <= v <= max_val]
        for val in possible:
            assignments[(x,y)] = val
            # verify any fully assigned L shapes
            ok=True
            shapes=[((x,y),(x,y+1),(x+1,y))]
            if x>0:
                shapes.append(((x-1,y),(x-1,y+1),(x,y)))
            if y>0:
                shapes.append(((x,y-1),(x,y),(x+1,y-1)))
            for p1,p2,p3 in shapes:
                if p1 in assignments and p2 in assignments and p3 in assignments:
                    vals=[assignments[p1],assignments[p2],assignments[p3]]
                    if len(set(vals))!=3 or max(vals)-min(vals)!=2:
                        ok=False
                        break
            if ok:
                backtrack(idx+1)
            del assignments[(x,y)]
    assignments[(0,0)]=0
    backtrack(1)
    return solutions

sols_01 = count_assignments(0,1, max_val=30)
len(sols_01), sorted({sol[(0,1)] for sol in sols_01})[:20], max({sol[(0,1)] for sol in sols_01})

[CODE] (turn 8):
def residue_assignments(N):
    # assign residues 0,1,2 for points (0..N,0..N)
    assignments={}
    points=[(x,y) for x in range(N+1) for y in range(N+1)]
    points.sort(key=lambda p:(p[0]+p[1],p[0]))
    solutions=[]
    def backtrack(idx):
        if idx==len(points):
            solutions.append(dict(assignments))
            return
        x,y=points[idx]
        # compute possible residues based on constraints where other two known
        possible_vals=None
        def constrain(a,b):
            nonlocal possible_vals
            if a==b:
                return False
            # need third residue distinct from a,b, i.e., the remaining element of {0,1,2}
            remaining = {0,1,2} - {a,b}
            if len(remaining)!=1:
                return False
            r=remaining.pop()
            if possible_vals is None:
                possible_vals={r}
            else:
                possible_vals=possible_vals.intersection({r})
                if not possible_vals:
                    return False
            return True
        # check shapes where this point is third
        if (x, y+1) in assignments and (x+1, y) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x, y+1)], assignments[(x+1, y)]):
                return
        if x>0 and (x-1, y) in assignments and (x-1, y+1) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x-1, y)], assignments[(x-1, y+1)]):
                return
        if y>0 and (x, y-1) in assignments and (x+1, y-1) in assignments:
            if not constrain(assignments[(x, y-1)], assignments[(x+1, y-1)]):
                return
        if possible_vals is None:
            possible_vals={0,1,2}
        for r in possible_vals:
            assignments[(x,y)]=r
            # verify fully assigned L shapes
            ok=True
            shapes=[((x,y),(x,y+1),(x+1,y))]
            if x>0:
                shapes.append(((x-1,y),(x-1,y+1),(x,y)))
            if y>0:
                shapes.append(((x,y-1),(x,y),(x+1,y-1)))
            for p1,p2,p3 in shapes:
                if p1 in assignments and p2 in assignments and p3 in assignments:
                    vals={assignments[p1],assignments[p2],assignments[p3]}
                    if vals!={0,1,2}:
                        ok=False
                        break
            if ok:
                backtrack(idx+1)
            del assignments[(x,y)]
    # set r(0,0)=0 w.l.o.g (global shift)
    assignments[(0,0)]=0
    backtrack(1)
    return solutions

sols_res=residue_assignments(3)
len(sols_res)

def pattern_of(sol):

    N=3

[CODE] (turn 13):
def possible_Ks(r):
    sols=[]
    for K0 in range(3):
        for K1 in range(3):
            for K2 in range(3):
                N0=3*K0+r
                N1=3*K1+r+1
                N2=3*K2+r+2
                vals=sorted([N0,N1,N2])
                if vals[2]-vals[0]==2 and len(set(vals))==3:
                    sols.append((K0,K1,K2,tuple(vals)))
    return sols

for r in range(3):
    print(r, possible_Ks(r)[:10])

[CODE] (turn 14):
def allowed_K_triplet():
    allowed=set()
    for K0 in range(3):
        for K1 in range(3):
            for K2 in range(3):
                # residues r,r+1,r+2 (choose r=0 for simplicity)
                N0=3*K0+0
                N1=3*K1+1
                N2=3*K2+2
                vals=sorted([N0,N1,N2])
                if vals[2]-vals[0]==2 and len(set(vals))==3:
                    allowed.add((K0,K1,K2))
    return allowed

allowed = allowed_K_triplet()
len(allowed), sorted(allowed)[:20]

[CODE] (turn 16):
def allowed_K_triplet_order(order):
    # order is list of residues offsets from base r: e.g., [0,1,2] or [0,2,1]
    allowed=set()
    for K0 in range(3):
        for K1 in range(3):
            for K2 in range(3):
                N0=3*K0+0
                N1=3*K1+order[1]
                N2=3*K2+order[2]
                vals=sorted([N0,N1,N2])
                if vals[2]-vals[0]==2 and len(set(vals))==3:
                    allowed.add((K0,K1,K2))
    return allowed

allowed_A=allowed_K_triplet_order([0,1,2])
allowed_B=allowed_K_triplet_order([0,2,1])
len(allowed_A), sorted(allowed_A)[:10], len(allowed_B), sorted(allowed_B)[:10]

[CODE] (turn 16):
def allowed_K_triplet_order(order):
    # order is list of residues offsets from base r: e.g., [0,1,2] or [0,2,1]
    allowed=set()
    for K0 in range(3):
        for K1 in range(3):
            for K2 in range(3):
                N0=3*K0+0
                N1=3*K1+order[1]
                N2=3*K2+order[2]
                vals=sorted([N0,N1,N2])
                if vals[2]-vals[0]==2 and len(set(vals))==3:
                    allowed.add((K0,K1,K2))
    return allowed

from collections import defaultdict

def possible_values_grid(max_x, max_y):
    # DP storing set of possible values for each point given constraints on subgrid up to that point
    # We'll store mapping from (x,y) to set of possible values that can appear at that point in some valid assignment on subgrid.
    # However need to consider interactions: the value at a point may affect future possibilities.
    # We'll store full assignments for small grid to compute exact reachable values.
    sols = count_assignments(max_x, max_y, max_val=20)  # small bound
    # collect possible values at each point
    vals = defaultdict(set)
    for sol in sols:
        for (x,y), v in sol.items():
            vals[(x,y)].add(v)
    return vals

vals_grid = possible_values_grid(3,3)
for pt in sorted(vals_grid):
    print(pt, sorted(vals_grid[pt])[:10], '... size', len(vals_grid[pt])

- c = g(x,y+1) = 3*min(s+1, Q) + r'', where r'' = (x - (y+1)) mod 3 = (r-1) mod 3.

Now we need to check that {a,b,c} are consecutive.

Case 1: s+1 ≤ Q (i.e., both neighboring cells have q = s+1). Then:

a = 3s + r (since s ≤ Q)
b = 3(s+1) + r' = 3s + 3 + r'
c = 3(s+1) + r'' = 3s + 3 + r''

Now r, r', r'' are residues r, r+1, r-1 (mod 3) which are a permutation of {0,1,2}. Specifically, r' = (r+1) mod 3, r'' = (r-1) mod 3.

- a = 3s + r
- b = 3s + 3 + (r+1 mod 3)
- c = 3s + 3 + (r-1 mod 3)

Since (r+1 mod 3) and (r-1 mod 3) are the other two residues.

Thus our earlier assumption that any q satisfying Lipschitz condition yields valid g is false; the residues pattern interacts with q values such that the triple must be consecutive, which imposes stricter constraints.

Let's analyze the condition more carefully.

Given residues r are fixed pattern (x - y mod 3). For a given L shape at (x,y), the residues are r, r+1, r-1 (mod 3). The values are:

a = 3q + r
b = 3q_r + (r+1) mod 3
c = 3q_c + (r-1) mod 3

Where q = q(x,y), q_r = q(x+1,y), q_c = q(x,y+1).

We need the set {a,b,c} to be consecutive. Let's order them.

Since residues are distinct, the numbers will be in three different residue classes. The consecutive numbers must be of the form 3k + 0, 3k+1, 3k+2 for some k (i.e., same block). Therefore the three numbers must share the same quotient k (i.e., floor division by 3). Thus we must have:

floor(a/3) = floor(b/3) = floor(c/3) = k.

Thus the condition is that the three q values must be such that after adding residues, the floor division is equal.

Given a = 3q + r, floor(a/3) = q if r<3 (always). Since 0≤r≤2, floor((3q+r)/3) = q.

Similarly, floor(b/3) = q_r (since r' is 0..2). So condition requires q = q_r = q_c = k. So all three q must be equal! Wait but earlier we saw example with linear solution where q values were not equal (e.g., at (0,1) we had q values 0,1,1). Let's compute residues there.

Take (0,1): coordinates (x=0,y=1). r = (0-1) mod3 = -1 mod3 = 2. So residues: r=2 at (0,1). At (1,1): r' = (1-1)=0. At (0,2): r'' = (0-2)=-2 mod3 =1.

Values: g(0,1)=2 (q=0,r=2). g(1,1)=3 (q=1,r=0). g(0,2)=4 (q=1,r=1). Indeed q values are 0,1,1 not equal. Yet the numbers are consecutive 2,3,4. Let's compute floor division: floor(2/3)=0, floor(3/3)=1, floor(4/3)=1. Not equal. So our deduction that floor must be equal is wrong. Because the three consecutive numbers can straddle a multiple of 3, causing one of them to have lower quotient.

Thus the condition on q is more subtle: The three numbers must be of the form (3k+r), (3k+r+1), (3k+r+2) for some k and r. This means that after sorting, the residues are 0,1,2, but the quotients are either all k, or two are k+1 and one is k, depending on where the block starts.

Specifically, if the smallest number has residue r (0,1,2) and quotient k, then the three numbers are:

- n = 3k + r
- n+1 = 3k + r + 1
- n+2 = 3k + r + 2

Now residues of these three numbers are (r, r+1, r+2) mod 3, which is a permutation of {0,1,2}. The quotients are:

- floor((3k+r)/3) = k
- floor((3k+r+1)/3) = k if r ≤ 1 else k+1? Let's compute: If r=0 => numbers 3k,3k+1,3k+2 => quotients k,k,k.
If r=1 => numbers 3k+1,3k+2,3k+3 => quotients k,k,k+1.
If r=2 => numbers 3k+2,3k+3,3k+4 => quotients k,k+1,k+1.

Thus the pattern of quotients depends on r (the residue of the smallest number). So the three q values (quotients) can be either all equal, or two equal (higher) and one lower, depending on which residue is missing at the lower end.

Now in our L shape, residues are (r, r+1, r-1) modulo 3, which is a permutation of {0,1,2}. The ordering of residues determines which one is smallest.

Thus the condition on q is: Among the three q values, either they are all equal, or the one corresponding to residue 0 (i.e., the smallest number) may have q = k, while the other two have q = k or k+1 depending.

But we can characterize more concretely: Let’s denote the residues at the three positions: r (at (x,y)), r+1 (mod3) at (x+1,y), r-1 (mod3) at (x,y+1). The actual numeric values are:

a = 3q + r
b = 3q_r + (r+1) mod3
c = 3q_c + (r-1) mod3

We need these to be consecutive. Let's consider the possible ordering of residues.

Since residues are distinct, the smallest value among a,b,c will be the one with residue that is smallest after adjusting for q differences.

We can examine cases based on relative q values.

But perhaps there is a simpler way: The condition can be expressed as: The multiset {q, q_r, q_c} must be either {k,k,k} or {k,k,k+1} or {k,k+1,k+1} where the assignment of which cell gets which q depends on residues.

Specifically, the cell with residue 0 (i.e., value congruent 0 mod 3) could be either the middle or largest depending on q.

Let's enumerate possibilities using the six patterns of residues.

We can attempt to compute allowed triples of (q, q_r, q_c) for each pattern of residues.

But perhaps we can find a simpler characterization: The function h(x,y) = floor((g(x,y) + s(x,y))/3 where s is some shift may be monotone.

Alternatively, we can think of g as representing a "height" of a plane partition with steps of size 1.

Maybe the condition is equivalent to requiring that the function g is a "discrete convex" function with slopes (1,2) or (2,1). Not sure.

Given complexity, maybe the earlier conclusion that any q between 0 and x+y is possible is still true, but we need to verify with more careful construction.

We can attempt to construct a function achieving any desired q0 at target using a "staircase" where q increases along a diagonal path and stays constant elsewhere, but we need to ensure the L shape condition.
